1	# Verify proposed fixes to LR explanations
2	
3	Another worker proposed edits to LSAT U's LR Question Explanations. You are the independent check. Nothing is applied unless you approve it. Be skeptical: your job is to catch edits that are wrong, not to admire them.
4	
5	## Inputs
6	- The proposals: a JSON list at the path you are given. Each has qid, table, row_id, column, style, letter (for notes), old, new, kind, why, evidence.
7	- Read-only mirror of the database: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/` (`lr_questions.json` has stimulus, prompt = stem, correct_choice; `lr_answer_choices.json` has choice_text, tag per letter; `lr_explanations.json`, `lr_explanation_steps.json`, `lr_explanation_choice_notes.json`).
8	- The bank students read: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu/public/data/PT{n}-S{s}-LR.json`.
9	Load JSON with Python (`python -I`), never whole files into context. Write scripts only in your work directory.
10	
11	## For every proposal
12	Read the question's stimulus, stem, all five choices, the key, and the old and new text in full. Then decide:
13	- **approve** — the new text is correct: every quote matches the choice or stimulus exactly, the logic is right for this question and key, the verdict for the letter agrees with the key (correct letter is argued as correct, wrong letters as wrong), nothing outside the defect was damaged, and it reads clearly.
14	- **fix** — the edit is the right idea but has a mistake you can correct. Give the full corrected `new`.
15	- **reject** — the edit is wrong, unnecessary, or makes things worse. Say why.
16	
17	Hard rules for approve/fix: no added em dashes; no added gendered pronouns (he, she, his, her...) for people in the stimulus; no mention of Logic Games; never change which choice is credited; a wrong-answer note must say why that choice is wrong, a correct-answer note why it is right.
18	
19	Also settle any open question the proposer raised in its `why` (for example a disputed verdict). If you cannot settle it with confidence, reject that edit and say what is needed.
20	
21	## Output
22	Write JSON Lines to the output path you are given, one per proposal, in input order:
23	`{"row_id": "...", "qid": "...", "verdict": "approve|fix|reject", "new": "<only when fix: full corrected text>", "note": "one sentence"}`
24	
25	Reply with counts of approve, fix, reject, and list every reject and fix with its one-sentence reason.
26

2	
3	You are fixing **real errors** in LSAT U's LR Question Explanations, the teaching students read after answering. You do NOT write to any database. You produce a proposal file. 5	## Read-only sources (local mirror of the live database, refreshed 2026-10-02/03)
6	Directory: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/`
7	- `lr_questions.json` — id, qid (e.g. PT146-S3-Q1), stimulus, prompt (the stem), correct_choice, question_type
8	- `lr_answer_choices.json` — question_id, letter, choice_text, tag, is_correct
9	- `lr_explanations.json` — id, question_id, style (`standard`, `brief`, `another_way`, `single`)
10	- `lr_explanation_steps.json` — id, explanation_id, position, label, body (the Argument Breakdown steps)
11	- `lr_explanation_choice_notes.json` — id, explanation_id, letter, body (the Choice Notes)
12	
13	Students see the first style present in the order standard, brief, another_way, single, and the selector lets them see the others. Fix the defect in **every style where it occurs**, not just one.

19	## What to do for each item on your list
20	1. Find the question and the row(s) the item describes. Read the stimulus, the stem, all five choices, the key, and the full explanation (all styles).
21	2. Confirm the defect is real. The list was written by earlier readers and may be wrong or already fixed. If it is not real, record a `skip` with the reason.
22	3. Write the smallest edit that makes the text correct:
23	   - Keep the author's voice, structure and wording everywhere outside the defect.
24	   - A misquote, slip or wrong-letter reference: correct the words.
25	   - Garbled or flattened transcription (scrambled diagrams, run-together words, truncation): reconstruct the intended text from the stimulus and the surrounding explanation. Put each diagram chain on its own line, with `→` arrows. If you cannot reconstruct it with confidence, rewrite only that sentence or chain plainly and correctly.
26	   - A note that never says why its choice is wrong or right (verdict-only, pasted from another letter, identical to another note, empty, placeholder): write a real note of 1–3 sentences that states exactly why, tied to the choice's own words and the stimulus.
27	   - Overbroad rules stated as fact ("this answer is always wrong"), mocking or political asides, Logic Games analogies, claims about answer placement: remove or soften them so the teaching point stands. There are no Logic Games on the current LSAT; never mention them.
28	   - Never change which choice is credited. If you believe the key itself is wrong, record a `flag`, not an edit.
29	4. New or rewritten text rules: no em dashes (use a comma, colon, or a new sentence); no gendered pronouns for people in the stimulus (use the name, "the author", "they"); plain, direct sentences; teach the reasoning, do not just assert it; no filler ("Great question", "it's important to note").
30	5. Double-check every proposal: re-read the edited text next to the choice text and stimulus, and confirm the logic and every quote are exact.

17	## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one
18	
19	Length does not. These do:
20	
21	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
22	2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
23	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
24	4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
25	5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.
26	
27	Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.

===== PT158-S2-Q2 Flaw key A
STIM: Council president: Councilmember Smith has proposed a new city ordinance prohibiting the use of the "bait-and-switch" sales tactic. Smith must have amnesia. Four years ago he owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch. Clearly, Smith's proposed ordinance does not even merit consideration.
STEM: The council president's reasoning is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the president
## Conclusion (Clearly)
Smith's proposed ordinance does not even merit consideration.
## Evidence
He's currently proposing a new city ordinance prohibiting "bait-and-switch", but four years ago he owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch.
## Evaluate
Whenever we're doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we consider the 2 of the 10 Famous Flaws that deal with rebuttals: Ad Hominem - This refers to when an author dismisses an argument because the source of the argument has a biased interest or conflicting past behavior. Unproven vs. Proven False - This refers to when an author argues that "because there's no evidence for X, or because someone's attempt to prove X failed, that shows that X is false". This looks to be an example of Ad Hominem. The author isn't saying that Smith's claim is false, so it couldn't be Unproven vs. Proven False. She's just dismissing his view, saying "it doesn't even merit consideration". And why? Because of Smith's conflicting past behavior. We should never reject someone's suggestion that we do Y just because that person used to say/do the opposite of Y. People and circumstances can change. Maybe there was someone who was really into cryptocurrency when it first came out but now is against it because of how bad its energy demands are for the environment. We can't say, "Don't listen to that crypto-critic. After all, he was way into Bitcoin when it first came out."
## Goal
Look for an answer describing this famous flaw; often it sounds like "the author dismisses an argument because of its source rather than its content". Otherwise, it might be a more specific answer that says, "because Person X did thing A in the past, we shouldn't listen to X's idea to do away with thing A."
-- A: This answer precisely identifies the argument's flaw. The council president dismisses the proposed ordinance entirely because of who proposed it — Smith, a former bait-and-switch practitioner — rather than because of any problem with the ordinance itself. The merits of a proposal are logically independent of the personal history of its author. Smith's past use of bait-and-switch might make him a hypocrite, but it says nothing about whether the ordinance is well-crafted, enforceable, or beneficial to consumers. By attacking the source rather than the content, the president avoids engaging with the substance of the proposal entirely. This is a classic ad hominem fallacy: the argument treats biographical information about the proposer as grounds for rejecting the proposition.
-- B: This answer describes using a single incompatible fact to dismiss a claim as false. But the argument does not work that way. The president is not identifying a fact that contradicts the ordinance and using it to prove the ordinance is wrong. Instead, the president is using a fact about Smith's personal history to dismiss the ordinance without evaluating it. The distinction matters: the incompatibility described in this answer would be between evidence and the claim being evaluated, but here the "evidence" (Smith's history) has no logical bearing on the cl...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

import sys
from pathlib import Path
sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]))
from project_paths import project_path, VAULT_ROOT
import json,glob,collections,re,sys
sys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding='utf-8')
DB='C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/'
BANK=str(project_path('lsatu-wt-glossary', 'public', 'data')) + '/' 
L=lambda t: json.load(open(DB+t+'.json',encoding='utf-8'))
qs=L('lr_questions'); ex=L('lr_explanations'); st=L('lr_explanation_steps'); nt=L('lr_explanation_choice_notes')
S=collections.defaultdict(list); N=collections.defaultdict(dict)
for s in st: S[s['explanation_id']].append(s)
for n in nt: N[n['explanation_id']][n['letter']]=n['body']
E={}
for e in ex:
  if e['style'] in('single','standard'): E[e['question_id']]=e
bank={}
for f in glob.glob(BANK+'PT*-S*-LR.json'):
  for b in json.load(open(f,encoding='utf-8')): bank[f"{b['practiceTest']}-S{b['sectionNumber']}-Q{b['questionNumber']}"]=b
def fam(t,stem=''):
  t=(t or '').lower(); s=(stem or '').lower()
  for k,v in [('parallel flaw','ParFlaw'),('parallel flawed','ParFlaw'),('parallel','Parallel'),('flaw','Flaw'),('paradox','Paradox'),('explain','Paradox'),('necessary','NA'),('sufficient','SA'),('method','Method'),('agree','AgreeDis'),('disagree','AgreeDis'),('principle-strengthen','PrinStr'),('principle strengthen','PrinStr'),('justif','PrinStr'),('principle','PrinConf'),('main','MC'),('role','Role'),('weaken','Weaken'),('strengthen','Strengthen'),('evaluate','Evaluate'),('must be false','MBF'),('must','MBT'),('support','MBT')]:
    if k in t: return v
  return 'Other'
def shape(text):
  t=(text or '').lower(); out=set()
  if re.search(r'\b(if|only if|unless|any|every|all|requires|must)\b',t): out.add('cond')
  if re.search(r'\b(cause|caused|because of|leads? to|result|effect|due to)\b',t): out.add('causal')

# Copy corrections

Every student-facing wording fix, with the reason it was wrong. Read this before
writing or editing copy for LSAT U so the same mistake is not made twice.

Companion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This
says what has actually gone wrong and why.

Started 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a
correction is made.

## The rules these corrections came from

Three rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was
enforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface
added later starts uncovered.

- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.
- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`
  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.
- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.

Guards now live with the content rather than with each component:
`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every
surface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.

## Corrections

### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)

Joshua's marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing

2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same r

A rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equiva

### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)

Joshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: "Read each whole idea," "Find what the statements guarantee," the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goa

##### PT158-S2-Q2
[Conclusion (Clearly)] Smith's proposed ordinance does not even merit consideration.
[Evidence] Smith is now proposing an ordinance that bans the "bait-and-switch" sales tactic. But four years ago, Smith owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch.
[Evaluate] Whenever a Flaw argument rejects someone's proposal, ask what the rejection is based on. Is it the proposal itself, or the person who made it?

The council president never says a word about the ordinance. Would it work? Would it protect shoppers? Is bait-and-switch harmful? None of that comes up. The only reason given is Smith's past: Smith's store was famous for the very tactic the ordinance would ban.

That is the Ad Hominem flaw: dismissing a position because of its source rather than its content. Smith's history might make Smith look like a hypocrite, but hypocrisy doesn't make an idea bad. Given that Smith once used bait-and-switch, how could we still argue the ordinance deserves a hearing? Easily. People change. Smith may have watched customers get burned and now wants the tactic gone. Someone who knows a tactic from the inside may even be well placed to write a rule against it.

Notice what the president does not say. The president never claims the ordinance is false or mistaken, only that it "does not even merit consideration." So answers about proving a claim false miss the target. The objection is to refusing to look at the ordinance at all.
[Goal] Look for an answer describing this famous flaw; often it sounds like "the author dismisses an argument because of its source rather than its content". Otherwise, it might be a more specific answer that says, "because Person X did thing A in the past, we shouldn't listen to X's idea to do away with thing A."
A This is the prediction almost word for word. The "source" is Smith, the person who proposed the ordinance, and the president's only reason is Smith's old store. The "content" is what the ordinance would actually do, which the president never discusses. Test it: could the ordinance still be a good law even though Smith once used bait-and-switch? Yes. So the president's reason never touches the ordinance. When every reason for rejecting an idea is about the person behind it, look for this source-versus-content wording.
B A single fact that truly contradicts a claim does show the claim is false, so this often isn't even a flaw. And the president never says the ordinance is false. Smith's old store is not incompatible with the ordinance being a good law; it is a fact about the person who wrote it.
C The president never discusses sales tactics at all, deceptive or legitimate. Nothing in the argument depends on what counts as bait-and-switch, so no distinction between kinds of tactics is needed. The argument fails because it looks only at who proposed the ordinance.
D Circular reasoning would mean the premise already states the conclusion. The premise is about Smith's store four years ago; the conclusion is that the ordinance does not merit consideration. Those are different claims. The premise is about the wrong thing, but it doesn't repeat the conclusion.
E There is no generalization here. The president moves from one fact about Smith to one conclusion about Smith's ordinance and never extends the point to other councilmembers, other ordinances, or bait-and-switch in general. With no sample and no broader category, a generalization flaw has nothing to describe.
##### PT119-S2-Q24
[Conclusion] The ancient Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages.
[Evidence] An Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. depicts what appears to be a brewery, and its chemical residue shows it held alcoholic beer. That is older than the Babylonian wine-making evidence from 1500 B.C., which had been thought to be the first.
[Evaluate] "First known" and "first ever" are different claims. The cup is the earliest evidence of alcohol we have found. That puts Egy...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

pilot={'PT158-S2-Q2','PT157-S2-Q12','PT112-S4-Q24','PT122-S1-Q9','PT119-S2-Q24','PT123-S3-Q23'}

3	('b8076a74-666d-40bb-83ae-1b88584557ef'::uuid,'Whenever we''re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we consider the 2 of the 10 Famous Flaws that deal with rebuttals: Ad Hominem - This refers to when an author dismisses an argument because the source of the argument has a biased interest or conflicting past behavior. Unproven vs. Proven False - This refers to when an author argues that "because there''s no evidence for X, or because someone''s attempt to prove X failed, that shows that X is false". This looks to be an example of Ad Hominem. The author isn''t saying that Smith''s claim is false, so it couldn''t be Unproven vs. Proven False. She''s just dismissing his view, saying "it doesn''t even merit consideration". And why? Because of Smith''s conflicting past behavior. We should never reject someone''s suggestion that we do Y just because that person used to say/do the opposite of Y. People and circumstances can change. Maybe there was someone who was really into cryptocurrency when it first came out but now is against it because of how bad its energy demands are for the environment. We can''t say, "Don''t listen to that crypto-critic. After all, he was way into Bitcoin when it first came out."','Whenever a Flaw argument rejects someone''s proposal, ask what the rejection is based on. 5	The council president never says a word about the ordinance. Would it work? Would it protect shoppers? Is bait-and-switch harmful? None of that comes up. The only reason given is Smith''s past: Smith''s store was famous for the very tactic the ordinance would ban.
6	
7	That is the Ad Hominem flaw: dismissing a position because of its source rather than its content. Smith''s history might make Smith look like a hypocrite, but hypocrisy doesn''t make an idea bad. Given that Smith once used bait-and-switch, how could we still argue the ordinance deserves a hearing? Easily. People change. Smith may have watched customers get burned and now wants the tactic gone. Someone who knows a tactic from the inside may even be well placed to write a rule against it.
8	
9	Notice what the president does not say. The president never claims the ordinance is false or mistaken, only that it "does not even merit consideration." So answers about proving a claim false miss the target. The objection is to refusing to look at the ordinance at all.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),

The council president dismisses the proposed ordinance entirely because of who proposed it — Smith, a former bait-and-switch practitioner — rather than because of any problem with the ordinance itself. The merits of a proposal are logically independent of the personal history of its author. Smith''s past use of bait-and-switch might make him a hypocrite, but it says nothing about whether the ordinance is well-crafted, enforceable, or beneficial to consumers. - **PT119-S2-Q12:** removed an em dash from the Evaluate step and made it explain that the heat, not the exercise, is the cause. The Goal now names the answer's shape. A, B, C and E were strengthened; D was kept.
- **PT125-S4-Q12:** added Evaluate and Goal steps and rewrote all five notes. The key point is that a hypothesis we can't test is unconfirmed, not probably false.
- **PT133-S2-Q12:** the old first step was the pasted stimulus. It is now a numbered breakdown, plus new Evaluate and Goal steps that separate what the commentator grants Winslow from what the "But" rejects. All five notes were rewritten, and C gets the longest one as the main trap.
- **PT134-S3-Q3:** added Evaluate and Goal steps showing how the two premises overlap. B, C, D and E were rewritten; A was kept.
- **PT135-S2-Q14:** added the "early times" limit to the diagrams, added Evaluate and Goal steps, and rewrote all notes. B now gets two counterexamples: a foraging society, and modern irrigation.
- **PT137-S4-Q15:** added an Evaluate step that chains through the contrapositive, and a Goal. All notes were rewritten. The old C note used a diagram that didn't match the choice; the new one says the 40 to 60 percent figure is required for the best compost but doesn't prove a compost is good.

**Skip:**
- **PT126-S3-Q16:** it already has a sound Statements, Evaluate and Goal breakdown, and every wrong-answer note gives a reason tied to its choice.

Every old step and note keeps its original row, and new steps have a null row. I checked every quote against the bank wording and scanned the output for em or en dashes and gendered pronouns; none remain.

The file passes the brief's mechanical checks:
- No em or en dashes, and no gendered pronouns.
- Every old step's `row_id` is kept and in its original position.
- Each item has exactly five notes, A to E, each reusing its letter's `row_id`.

Three items (PT157-S3-Q14, PT157-S3-Q17, PT157-S3-Q9) had no prediction step, so each gained a new Goal step with a null `row_id`.

Every one of the eight was in the old PT157/158 register, with em dashes and padded, untested wrong-answer notes. Beyond that, these items had real problems in the old text:
- **PT157-S2-Q9:** the old Evaluate and Goal were aimed at the wrong flaw (side effects on overall learning). The rewrite is built around the actual gap: no evidence that month-long breaks avoid the same forgetting.
- **PT158-S2-Q18:** the old Evaluate and Goal chased the word "necessary" instead of the real gap. The rewrite uses a worked example: one practitioner gets 50% on sprains and 12% on fractures, the other the reverse, so both average 31% while the choice matters a lot for any one patient. It also fixes a typo in the Evidence step ("or a by a specialist"). The packet's known problem (the A note "argues against a claim A does not make") is stale: that note is accurate, and I kept its logic. The real fault was the E note, which brought in an outside "specialists take harder cases" story; the rewrite fixes that.
- **PT158-S2-Q14:** the old Evaluate mocked the argument and used "she" throughout. Both are gone.
- **PT157-S3-Q9:** the first-person wine joke is replaced with a "drive traffic to a web page" analogy.
- **PT158-S2-Q10:** the D note on the "third civilization" trap now runs the test on it. Even if a third civilization invented the style, the conclusion still holds, so the argument never needed to rule one out.

Two items the verifier should look at closely:
1. **PT158-S2-Q18, choice C:** I eliminate it because the evidence is records of whether patients improved, not patients' opinions of their practitioners. That reading of "Records reveal" is sound, but someone could argue the records might be patient-reported.
2. **PT157-S2-Q9, intermediate conclusion:** I kept the old label "Intermediate Conclusion" on the "will insure" claim, though the word "since" could also make it a premise. Only the Evaluate and Goal bodies changed in that item.

My quote check flagged a few quoted phrases that don't appear in the question. Each one is one of my own paraphrases ("fails to consider", "more acts are now called crimes", and the like), not a misquote of the stimulus or choices. Every phrase quoted from the question matches the bank wording.

<result>I wrote proposals for all 8 qids to `S3/out-06.jsonl`: **8 rewrite, 0 skip, 0 flag**. I agree with the key on every one. Nothing was written to any database.

| qid | Type | Key | Main fixes |
|---|---|---|---|
| PT158-S2-Q13 | Main Conclusion | B | Removed the "She" in Evaluate; filled out the one-line Goal; replaced the em-dash notes with support-test notes, longest on D (last-claim trap) |
| PT158-S3-Q10 | Main Conclusion | B | Evidence had dropped "most" from the decay premise; Evaluate rewritten with a clean diagram; Goal now quotes the actual sentence |
| PT158-S3-Q3 | Main Conclusion | D | Goal used "she" for the columnist; B note now explains the swap of "not required" for "not enough" with a test |
| PT158-S4-Q10 | Main Conclusion | A | The old B note called "automation means fewer workers" a supported intermediate conclusion, which the stimulus never supports; also removed the overbroad claim that Main Conclusion questions never use "therefore"-type words |
| PT158-S4-Q8 | Main Conclusion | C | Evaluate said "first case" for "first sentence" and never ran the support test; the repeated "building blocks / edifice" notes are replaced with ones tied to each choice's wording, with B (overstates "not justified" as "false") as the trap |
| PT117-S3-Q8 | Method | A | The notes called Rossi "he" throughout; Evaluate and Goal were one line each |
| PT119-S4-Q2 | Method | B | A and E notes were bare verdicts; added the "argues X by Y" test (X must match the conclusion, Y the evidence) |
| PT158-S3-Q22 | Method | C | The Conclusion step said "does not provide any grounds," stronger than the stimulus's "insufficient reason"; Goal had a typo ("ti") |

Every step kept its `row_id` and its 0-based position, with no steps added. Steps that were already sound stay word for word. Each item has exactly five notes, each reusing its row. My build script checked every step and note: no em or en dashes, and no gendered pronouns.

Three things to know:
- **The `known_problem` notes for PT117-S3-Q8 and PT119-S4-Q2 are stale.** They say all five choice notes are missing, but both now have notes. I rewrote them anyway because of the pronoun problem and the bare verdicts.
- **I dropped two "most popular wrong answer" claims** (PT117 D, PT119 C). I had no data to confirm them. The longest trap notes elsewhere are my own judgment of which answer tempts most, not real answer-pick numbers.
- **PT158-S3-Q10 Evaluate keeps the general "the last sentence is rarely the answer" rule**, and PT158-S4-Q8 keeps its "90% of the time" heuristic from the house text. They're stated as rules of thumb, not data, but a strict verifier may want them checked.

The script is `S3/work-06/build.py`.</result>

2	('9bfcc23e-1bbd-44ba-962f-dadd6f3f30d1'::uuid,'causal (by, which has enabled, because, increased)
3	electrical >> play w/ >> avg # of musicians instruments more volume per band decreased
4	pivot (nevertheless)
5	electrical >> greater # of professional instruments musicians','Causal chain:
6	Electrified instruments → each musician plays louder → fewer musicians per band, on average
7	
8	Pivot (nevertheless):
9	Electrified instruments → more musicians playing popular music professionally','Facts','Facts'),
10	('981bba23-7b58-4924-a7f4-1cb69e2341a9'::uuid,'The #1 thing we read for on Most Supported is Cause / Effect relationships, and we got four of them here.
11	There''s a causal chain, and then a pivot that creates a **mathy tension** between two different things caused by electrification: 1. fewer musicians per band 2. more musicians overall When we see a Pivot creating a mathy tension, the correct answer usually addresses / resolves that tension.
12	If we have fewer musicians in each band nowadays, how can also have more musicians total?
13	We would need there to be more bands than before.
14	Maybe there used to be 10 ppl / band, and there were 80 bands, giving us 800 musicians.
15	If we only have 5 ppl / band now and want to end up with 1000 musicians (some larger number), then we''d need 200 bands.','On a Most Supported question, cause and effect is the first thing to read for, and this paragraph is full of it: electrification enabled more volume, the extra volume shrank the average band, and electrification also raised the number of professional musicians.
16	
17	Then the pivot word "Nevertheless" sets two results of electrification against each other:
18	1. Fewer musicians per band, on average.
19	2. More professional musicians overall.
20	
21	When a pivot creates a math tension like this, the correct answer usually resolves it. So ask: if each band has fewer people, how can there be more people in total?
22	
23	There have to be more bands. Run numbers. Say there used to be 80 bands of 10, which is 800 musicians. If bands now average 5 and we have 1,000 musicians, we need 200 bands. Smaller bands plus more musicians forces the number of bands up.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
24	('777c8a66-eae6-4c4c-8411-1e960acb9087'::uuid,'The correct answer will probably reward us for understanding the causal chain between electrified instruments and fewer people per band, or it''ll test this mathy inference that there are more bands.','Look for an answer that says the number of bands has gone up. A safe answer could also simply restate the chain from electrification to smaller bands, but the math inference about more bands is the one the paragraph is built to test.','Goal','Goal'),
25	('e58b20ed-9724-4eb6-9e88-0a23a49369da'::uuid,'The researcher is offering a mechanism: a slight rise in body temperature lasting until after bedtime causes deeper sleep. Anything that produces that same condition (slight body-temperature rise persisting until bedtime) should produce deeper sleep too — even if it is not exercise.','The study gives us a result: afternoon exercisers got 33 percent more deep sleep than the control group. Then the researcher explains why: afternoon exercise raises body temperature slightly until after bedtime, and "this extra heat induces deeper sleep."
26	
27	That last clause is the key. The researcher does not say exercise itself deepens sleep. The researcher says the extra heat does. 28	('9d4092fb-98d3-4c44-aa6a-00bd3cb999c9'::uuid,'Find an answer that the mechanism statement directly supports. Be wary of answers that overstate (e.g., "necessary," "best way," "no one"). The right answer should be a modest extension of the mechanism, not...

2	('87919b24-e7fb-42a1-b12d-7f8ce5f8d72e'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','No conditional or causal chain here. The useful part is the pivot and its reason. The linguist is not attacking the hypothesis as false. The complaint is about verifiability: it is not clear the hypothesis could be tested at all.
3	
4	What follows from a hypothesis that may not be testable? We cannot confirm it, and we cannot refute it. So we are in no position to say whether it is true. Notice what does not follow: that it is false, or that it should be ignored. The linguist says nothing about either.'),
5	('87919b24-e7fb-42a1-b12d-7f8ce5f8d72e'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a modest answer saying we cannot currently tell whether the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis is true. 6	('979d1f49-6d4d-4f2a-a294-59fd314c0fbd'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','This is a concede-then-pivot paragraph, and the hard part is sorting what the commentator accepts from what the commentator rejects.
7	
8	Winslow makes a claim about editors: they publish antienvironmental articles because those articles seem daring, seeming to challenge prevailing positions. The commentator says "It is true" and repeats that point almost word for word. So the commentator accepts Winslow''s account of what editors prefer.
9	
10	Then comes "But." The pivot does not deny the editors'' preference. It denies that the articles actually are daring. Environmentalism is not the orthodox position, so attacking it is not a real challenge to orthodoxy, and the critics are not real dissidents. In short: the articles only seem daring, and that appearance is what editors are buying.
11	
12	On a hard Most Supported question, the conceded part is often where the right answer hides, because the pivot pulls attention away from it.'),
13	('979d1f49-6d4d-4f2a-a294-59fd314c0fbd'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Winslow is right about the editors: they prefer articles that seem to challenge prevailing positions. 14	('81205ffa-816c-4c4f-8466-43873dfa8ead'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','On a logical completion, the blank is the conclusion, so we ask what the premises are building toward.
15	
16	Look for the overlap. Premise one connects engine noise to communication: the noise sits in the whales'' communication range. Premise two connects engine noise to hearing damage. Put them together: boat noise can damage the very hearing the whales use to communicate.
17	
18	The "Though" clause matters too. The whales do not act differently around engines, which might make us think the noise is harmless. The author is warning against that: no visible reaction does not mean no harm.'),
19	('81205ffa-816c-4c4f-8466-43873dfa8ead'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a conclusion like "boat engine noise may harm the whales'' ability to communicate, even though they show no reaction." Keep it soft, since the premises only say the damage "can" happen, and keep it tied to communication.'),

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('b13f2d7f-892c-438a-a4db-4433f79e009e'::uuid,'be6d8383a6695a6e0ad133cc9202a95a','Go claim by claim through Paula''s argument and ask of each one: does Ashley push back?
3	
4	1. Earthlike planets are common, so there probably are intelligent beings on other planets. Ashley never touches this. Calling the search a waste doesn''t mean nothing is out there. It can just mean we''re listening for the wrong signal.
5	
6	2. Alien scientists would understand math and physics the way we do, so they would inevitably develop technologies such as radio communication. Ashley rejects this directly. Our science, mathematics, and technology are "unique outgrowths" of our physiology, cognitive makeup, and environment. Different beings, different outgrowths. The legal and political systems comparison is there to make Paula''s "inevitably" sound as odd as expecting aliens to have our courts and juries.
7	
8	3. We can expect our radio telescopes to detect signs of such beings. Ashley rejects this too: the search is "a waste of time and money."
9	
10	So the friction sits in claims 2 and 3, and they share one core question: would intelligent aliens end up with radio? Paula says inevitably. Ashley says there''s no reason to expect it.
11	
12	Underneath, Paula treats math and physics as something any intelligent mind would arrive at. Ashley treats them as a product of being human.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
13	('d03bb8ee-aa3f-48a6-bbe2-12ed75b7372a'::uuid,'725626240c027adec99de8fb27a5d15d','Look for an answer about whether intelligent aliens would have developed radio communication, or, nearly the same thing, whether radio telescopes are a sensible way to look for them. Paula has to land on yes and Ashley on no.
14	
15	Be careful with answers about intelligent life alone. Ashley never says whether aliens exist, so an answer about existence gives us an Agree position from Paula and nothing from Ashley.','Goal','Goal'),
16	('48e638a9-3a7e-490f-b2af-8569e906daf9'::uuid,'4bb6ba9ee22d1a012843172a1e94151f','Archaeologists can study the artifacts left by ancient cultures to determine whether they were nomadic or sedentary.
17	
18	Does Kendall push back on this? Yes, at least partly. Kendall says what artifacts a people make is determined largely by the materials available to them. If materials drive what gets made, the artifacts may tell us more about the local supply of stone and wood than about whether the people moved around. That makes the artifact test much less reliable.','Robin''s 1st Claim','Robin''s 1st Claim'),
19	('24ec064d-ffc5-4f19-b19f-d7442d3655c0'::uuid,'3985510ed1ddcfe30e9c3382a7fd8025','If artifacts were made to last, rather than to be quickly discarded, then the culture was likely sedentary.
20	
21	Does Kendall deny this exact claim? Not cleanly. Kendall''s point lands hardest on the flip side. Picture a settled village with nothing around it but reeds and soft wood. Everything it makes falls apart within a few years. Robin''s test would call that village nomadic, and Kendall''s point explains why that would be a mistake: the flimsy artifacts reflect the materials, not a life on the move.
22	
23	So Kendall might accept "made to last, so probably sedentary" while rejecting "not durable, so probably nomadic."','Robin''s 2nd Claim','Robin''s 2nd Claim'),
24	('eb15399b-1322-4d8c-88f7-888ae61f1933'::uuid,'189655fed1636dee9a9a7abcbb23ae0a','Robin is committed to more than the durable half. The first claim says artifacts can determine whether a culture was nomadic or sedentary, so Robin''s test has to sort both ways: lasting artifacts point to sedentary, throwaway artifacts point to nomadic.
25	
26	Kendall''s response targets that second half. Look for an answer about whether non-durable artifacts give us good reason to call a culture nomadic. Robin says yes. Kendall says no, because the available materials may explain the flimsiness...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

2	('910ca695-c885-4ed5-9bb8-2c4c9e249e5e'::uuid,'980807a53b1c94b0f48190d544225874','This combines the two claims Ashley attacks. Paula says there probably are intelligent beings on other planets and that they would "inevitably" develop technologies "such as radio communication." So Paula agrees it is probable that such beings exist and have radio.
3	
4	Ashley disagrees. If our technology is a unique outgrowth of human physiology, cognitive makeup, and environment, there''s no reason to expect aliens to have radio. That''s exactly why Ashley calls the radio-telescope search a waste.
5	
6	The move to repeat: find the claim the second speaker actually attacks, then pick the answer that restates it. Here that''s radio, not alien life itself.'),
7	('3d1872e8-5096-4a7b-ae9d-e8b6ff0b126e'::uuid,'675189f3a3d47dc6d13dd8ad88c2a14b','Paula says nothing about alien legal or political systems, so there''s no Agree position. Ashley raises them only as a comparison: assuming aliens share our physics is as unjustified as assuming they share our laws. A disagreement needs both speakers on record, and Paula is silent here.'),
8	('c08c751b-64fc-4535-acc4-b89cc0ce9b41'::uuid,'accd0a5d7d096c5eca0e53617ca03d46','Ashley agrees: technology is an "outgrowth" of our cognitive makeup. But can we find a Disagree position from Paula? No. Paula''s claim is about an outcome, that aliens would reach radio. Paula could grant that cognitive makeup influences technology and still think any intelligent species would get to radio anyway.'),
9	('59486f59-4475-4f97-8488-483d043fdbb5'::uuid,'086ee8a24f7f0072c378f9c74b5704fc','This is the tempting one, because Paula clearly agrees: there "probably are intelligent beings on other planets." Now look for Ashley''s Disagree position. Ashley attacks the method, radio telescopes, and the claim that aliens would share our science. Nothing Ashley says denies that intelligent life exists. Ashley''s objection makes perfect sense even if the aliens are out there and simply never built radios. With no Disagree position, D fails.'),
10	('418051c8-0731-4c10-af35-1233f66a84c7'::uuid,'dd9d9997b1b28646b846ff5d031f82b4','Paula never says scientists should spend more. Ashley''s side is shaky too: calling a radio search "a waste of time and money" rejects one method, and Ashley could still back other ways of searching. Neither speaker is clearly on record.'),
11	('604339a1-b41d-4bc1-a2ec-5548d1327a84'::uuid,'313bd49d4287b88498bd3cb69ab2dfbc','Robin plainly accepts the nomadic/sedentary distinction. Does Kendall call it illicit? No. Kendall never says the categories are confused or improper, only that artifacts are a shaky way to sort cultures into them. Doubting a test is not the same as rejecting the categories it sorts into, so there''s no Disagree position.'),
12	('4523229d-3b31-4f8e-9b66-0b471170567c'::uuid,'872a850cb2fd67284c7838f5ac521ef7','Robin agrees. The first claim says artifacts can tell us whether a culture was nomadic or sedentary, and the second ties lasting artifacts to sedentary life. Put together, artifacts that were not made to last point toward nomadic.
13	
14	Kendall disagrees. If materials largely determine what a people make, a settled culture with only flimsy materials would leave non-durable artifacts too. Then assuming "nomadic" from non-durable artifacts isn''t reasonable.
15	
16	Notice that B isn''t Robin''s second claim word for word. It''s the other half of the test that Robin''s first claim commits Robin to, and it''s the half Kendall''s point actually breaks. Ask which claim the second speaker''s objection would make false, then match that claim.'),

2	('6e5e2f99-16fd-4746-94d4-54fbd32182f9'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Beck overlooks that the program could produce consistent figures that are still inaccurate. That''s the scale that is always five pounds off.
3	
4	Watch for the reversed wording. Beck assumes consistent figures could only come from accurate estimates. An answer saying Beck assumes the output would stay consistent even if inaccurate has it backwards.'),
5	('73c328e3-c57e-44eb-bba9-8421e3101d46'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the argument overlooks that most mammals with short olecranon processes might not be predators. That''s the reversal in plain words: knowing most predators have short OPs doesn''t tell us most short-OP mammals are predators.
6	
7	Quick numbers. Say there are 10 predator species and 100 non-predator species. Every predator has a short OP, and so do 30 of the non-predators. Most predators have short OPs, but most short-OP mammals (30 of 40) are not predators. A short-OP mammal would then more likely be a non-predator than a predator, so Meg''s short OP wouldn''t make predation probable.'),
8	('e584e358-2f47-439a-a3db-cd92322c75ac'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the columnist takes for granted that subjects'' acceptance before the test holds up after they learn their substance was ineffective. Or the "fails to consider" version: subjects can accept a risk in advance and still resent it when it happens.')
9	) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;

input: {"project_id":"53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76","sql":"insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values\n('6e5e2f99-16fd-4746-94d4-54fbd32182f9'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Beck overlooks that the program could produce consistent figures that are still inaccurate. That''s the scale that is always five pounds off.\n\nWatch for the reversed wording. Beck assumes consistent figures could only come from accurate estimates. An answer saying Beck assumes the output would stay consistent even if inaccurate has it backwards.'),\n('73c328e3-c57e-44eb-bba9-8421e3101d46'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the argument overlooks that most mammals with short olecranon processes might not be predators. That''s the reversal in plain words: knowing most predators have short OPs doesn''t tell us most short-OP mammals are predators.\n\nQuick numbers. Say there are 10 predator species and 100 non-predator species. Every predator has a short OP, and so do 30 of the non-predators. Most predators have short OPs, but most short-OP mammals (30 of 40) are not predators. A short-OP mammal would then more likely be a non-predator than a predator, so Meg''s short OP wouldn''t make predation probable.'),\n('e584e358-2f47-439a-a3db-cd92322c75ac'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the columnist takes for granted that subjects'' acceptance before the test holds up after they learn their substance was ineffective. Or the \"fails to consider\" version: subjects can accept a risk in advance and still resent it when it happens.')\n) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;"}
[/external_agent_tool_call]

2	('cd1342fe-bb3b-469c-a6a4-750b15d91103'::uuid,'833907d7a11daa688e3a71cf1a2e3b38','This isn''t a crazy argument. The author is assuming that what happened in the recent past will probably happen again in the near future.
3	
4	Given that September beat July in each of the last two years, how could we argue that July might still get more rain this year? Easily. Two years is a tiny record. Two seasons can line up that way by chance, the way a fair coin can land heads twice in a row. And maybe something about this year is different from the last two.
5	
6	Notice that the author was careful: the conclusion says "probably," not "definitely." That softer wording helps, but it doesn''t save the argument. Even "probably" needs more than two data points behind it. The flaw is the size of the record, not the strength of the claim.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
7	('f84a9bed-d622-4d14-9f81-6f71996ab903'::uuid,'59b658d134acad3a5d7513e71f1a073a','Look for an answer saying the prediction rests on too few past cases: two years is not enough to say what this year will do. An answer could also say the author overlooks that this year may differ in some important way from the previous two. Expect abstract wording, so be ready to translate "future event" and "instances of related past events" back into this year''s rain and the last two years.','Goal','Goal'),
8	('ab7e94a7-ddbd-4d45-9caf-4cb90af52647'::uuid,'f52e0550ab91b0ffcffe95dcaa682d50','There are exceptions to the general rule that people should practice what they preach. Rephrase: sometimes it''s fine for people not to act the way they tell others to act.','Conclusion','Conclusion'),
9	('d9b0a365-ab76-41ff-b451-3a338496b16a'::uuid,'38823ceea03e15ca139d751d4f48b4cc','Logicians don''t need to be logical in their discussions of logic, any more than physicians need healthy lifestyles in order to treat people.','Evidence (for instance)','Evidence (for instance)'),
10	('fc848127-25d4-473d-8483-fd17e81006ed'::uuid,'89eb980c119dabe5b2090f845b588692','The author''s example of an exception is an analogy. Physicians preach health, and nobody thinks a physician has to live a healthy life to treat patients well. So, says the author, logicians don''t have to be logical in their discussions of logic either.
11	
12	Any time an argument rests on an analogy, ask: are these two cases alike in the way that matters? The physician case works. A physician''s lifestyle happens outside the job. A physician who never exercises can still diagnose and prescribe correctly.
13	
14	The logician case is different. Being logical while discussing logic isn''t something a logician does after work. It is the work. A logician who argues illogically about logic is doing the job badly. How would anyone come away understanding logic if the lesson itself were illogical?
15	
16	So, given the physician example, how could we argue the logician example still fails? Point out that the physician''s failure is personal, while the logician''s failure is professional.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
17	('1a1ff027-bd4f-45ee-a3cb-3be320682ecd'::uuid,'11f10060dd9db32537ab5739863deb07','Look for an answer that breaks the analogy: being illogical in discussions of logic is a failure at the logician''s actual job, while an unhealthy lifestyle doesn''t stop a physician from treating people well. The stem says "fails to take into account," so the right answer will be a fact that, had the author considered it, would weaken the comparison.','Goal','Goal'),
18	('542f69a1-1945-43b7-8afc-9f50da21f625'::uuid,'6ff67bc1b8d98623183ee0476522d562','The professor said A''s would go only to papers whose conclusions were supported by reliable statistical evidence. 2	('715bd9b1-bdba-4a58-b4c1-347624aa2904'::uuid,'e3e88d0c67d742f1033dc45a6d47e638','Circular reasoning means the premise already assumes the conclusion, so the argument proves nothing new. Here the premise is about the last two years and the conclusion is about this year. Those are different claims: the premise could be true and this year could still go the other way. The evidence is thin, but it is real evidence.'),
3	('801a0124-4d13-4853-97cf-dfd866f167cd'::uuid,'b988d795e2140ea0458c2836b0abd4a0','Translate the abstract words back into the argument. The phrase "an inference about a future event" is the prediction about this year''s rain. The phrase "a very limited number of instances of related past events" is the two previous years. That matches our prediction exactly: two years is too small a record to support even a "probably" about this year.
4	
5	The move to repeat: when an argument predicts the future from the past, count the past cases. If there are only a handful, look for an answer about a small or limited sample, often worded abstractly like this one.'),
6	('c771334e-fe3c-4b6f-b518-b8f60f1d0252'::uuid,'0f762642be81852308b93d584b5884a3','The argument never mentions averages or one unusually wet year, so it can''t overemphasize that possibility. 9	('a97b10c9-de1c-47e4-be9b-a95dfa7610e7'::uuid,'692a376ae27eb753ab6f8cd30a002c5a','This is the broken analogy we predicted. A logician who is illogical in discussions of logic is failing at the core of the job, which is incompetence. A physician with an unhealthy lifestyle can still treat people well. 10	
11	Run the stem''s test. The phrase "fails to take into account" means ask whether the idea would weaken the argument. It does. If the logician''s failure is incompetence and the physician''s isn''t, the physician example can''t earn logicians the same pass.
12	
13	The move to repeat: on an analogy argument, the right Flaw answer usually names the relevant difference between the two cases.'),

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('1797f203-7405-4af3-b631-02670691d960'::uuid,'11d53cd0483de31691e97a51f3a20044','Antonio:
3	1. One can live a life of moderation by never deviating from the middle course.
4	2. But never deviating has a cost: you lose the joy of spontaneity and miss the opportunities that come to people who occasionally take great chances or go too far.
5	
6	Marla:
7	1. Someone who never risks going too far is actually failing to live a life of moderation.
8	2. Reason: one must be moderate even in one''s moderation.','Antonio / Marla','Antonio and Marla'),
9	('1ad1e6b3-76c4-4914-ae71-a90f8924b362'::uuid,'fcc14f2192ad76164d677b3469b68c86','Go claim by claim and ask: does Marla disagree?
10	
11	Antonio''s claim 1: never deviating from the middle course is a way to live a life of moderation. Marla says the opposite. A person who never risks going too far is "failing to live a life of moderation." That''s a direct clash.
12	
13	Antonio''s claim 2: never deviating means missing out on spontaneity and opportunity. Marla doesn''t push back. If anything, Marla''s view leaves room for the occasional risk too, since moderation itself has to be practiced in moderation, which means sometimes stepping off the middle course.
14	
15	So they mostly agree that some risk is worth taking. Where they split is the definition. Antonio thinks unbroken middle-course living still counts as a life of moderation, even if it''s a dull one. Marla thinks it doesn''t count at all.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
16	('f06da175-a31e-4b96-a95e-1f79bbad878b'::uuid,'79a81c63d7c11222863a5a1b656d0aab','Lutsina:
17	1. Futuristic science fiction doesn''t need to represent current social realities.
18	2. So its writers can envisage radically new social arrangements.
19	3. Conclusion: it has the potential to be a richer source of social criticism than conventional fiction.
20	
21	Priscilla:
22	1. Science fiction writers envisage new technologies more skillfully than new social arrangements.
23	2. That shows writers'' imaginations are constrained by current realities.
24	3. Conclusion: the most effective social criticism comes from faithfully presenting current social realities, as conventional fiction does.','Lutsina / Priscilla','Lutsina and Priscilla'),
25	('fc040d7f-1735-467c-bfe3-f4d7ac1ce2c0'::uuid,'75231962662860b30d111e97669fdde1','Line the conclusions up. Lutsina says futuristic science fiction has the potential to be a richer source of social criticism than conventional fiction. Priscilla says the most effective social criticism comes from conventional fiction. Those two claims rank the same two genres in opposite order on the same job. That''s the disagreement.
26	
27	Now check the support. Lutsina''s reason is freedom: science fiction can leave current realities behind. Priscilla answers that the freedom is less real than it looks, because writers stay constrained by current realities. Notice what Priscilla doesn''t say. Priscilla never claims science fiction writers can''t envisage new social arrangements, only that they do it less skillfully than they envisage new technology. So don''t expect the answer to be about whether anyone has succeeded. Expect it to be about which genre is the better source of social criticism.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
28	('c8c0fd62-38a4-45af-ac32-f074fd153498'::uuid,'1d4acdeb9ff4a2fcc142d06d1876f93c','Graham:
29	1. A computer defeated the world''s chess champion.
30	2. So any type of human intellectual activity governed by fixed principles can be mastered by machines.
31	3. So a truly intelligent machine will inevitably be devised.
32	
33	Adelaide:
34	1. The computer was simply an extension of the people who programmed it.
35	2. It was the programmers'' successful distillation of the principles of chess that let them defeat the champion using a computer.','Graham / Adelaide','Graham and Adelaide'),
36	('4c5204e4-b044-4185-b590-9f832ae67e3...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

2	('5c91cbf0-d42a-460f-a52e-515517709794'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer about what counts as a life of moderation, something like: whether a person who never leaves the middle course is living a life of moderation. Expect it in general terms. Be wary of answers about whether risk or spontaneity is good, since both speakers seem to like a little of it.'),
3	('a4bdc902-10bf-4039-b888-3fcface276e4'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for: whether futuristic science fiction or conventional fiction is the better, or more promising, source of social criticism. Lutsina picks science fiction; Priscilla picks conventional fiction.'),
4	('644767bf-220e-477d-bd9c-ecfc58f280cd'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for: whether the win over the chess champion belongs to the computer or to the people who programmed it.'),
5	('c8f7f015-04cf-4f30-b059-16e7d3f7511b'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a claim Tate supports and Wong''s "all countries" rules out: some countries are better off as autocracies than as democracies.')
6	) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;

2	('ca91a43a-73c7-4337-b1b4-5d65a85fb0cd'::uuid,'9d8209771525c9fa4ed556912da13234','Both speakers accept this, so it can''t be the disagreement. Antonia says the market "is often volatile" but "provides the opportunity to make a large profit quickly." Maria opens with "I agree" on the quick profits, and "just as likely to take a huge loss" only adds to the volatility. Two people on the same side of a claim aren''t disagreeing about it.'),
3	('3c0aa30a-5d94-452e-9e32-64f25c50bf96'::uuid,'0c7c5deb5e55b03374b5f792d411c25f','Neither speaker says savings bonds give a large return. Maria praises bonds for a "constant, reliable income," which is about steadiness, not size. Antonia never mentions bonds at all. With no claim about the size of the return from either speaker, this can''t be the point they disagree on.'),
4	('80308a7d-1bc1-41f2-a2e9-5ac296b36dbf'::uuid,'8c12697b64e27fc4e7c647a1dbd79976','This is the tempting one, because Maria clearly agrees: stocks risk a huge loss, and bonds pay a constant, reliable income. Now test Antonia. Does Antonia think the stock market is safer? No. Antonia admits the market "is often volatile" and calls it the best place to invest anyway. Best and safest are different things. Antonia could agree that bonds are safer and still prefer stocks for the upside. Only one speaker takes a side here.'),
5	('00fdd75c-6f92-4738-b116-ea54f1fe6684'::uuid,'3b428c736a3403acf1b47b6a6ab25781','Run both tests. Maria AGREES: Maria grants the quick profits but chooses savings bonds for their "constant, reliable income," because the market carries an equal chance of a huge loss. That''s picking reliable income over the riskier shot at a big, fast profit. Antonia DISAGREES: Antonia admits the volatility and still calls the stock market "the best place to invest your money" because of the chance at a large, quick profit. That''s picking the riskier opportunity.
6	
7	Antonia never says "reliable income," and you don''t need the exact words. When both speakers grant the same facts, look for the disagreement in what each one does with them. Here they weigh the same tradeoff and land on opposite sides.'),
8	('cb3f070d-412c-49c6-bc57-107b5491fddb'::uuid,'11bb6401551203beee9f916f2180b77f','Test both positions. Antonio values occasional great chances: never taking them means one "misses the opportunities." Marla doesn''t object. Marla says a moderate life must sometimes risk going too far, so Marla seems to value the occasional chance too. Both lean the same way, so there''s no disagreement here.'),
9	('f66bca41-4b1b-4cd5-a668-68df25932c3d'::uuid,'0cbfe9e8130558bce201ec33a3f51dcc','This names the definitional clash. Antonio says never deviating from the middle course is enough for a life of moderation. Marla says someone who never risks going too far is "failing to live a life of moderation." So they give different answers to what moderation requires: for Antonio, nothing more than staying in the middle; for Marla, occasionally leaving it.
10	
11	The answer is abstract, so translate it back. What does a life of moderation require? For these two speakers, that becomes: is never deviating enough? Antonio says yes, Marla says no. When an answer is general, plug each speaker''s specific claim into it and check that you get one yes and one no.'),
12	('93bfb01f-501a-4983-9028-53a5635d57ac'::uuid,'b5b08cf28f1376d039999da10a819a37','Neither speaker mentions other virtues. Antonio talks about spontaneity and opportunity as things that never deviating costs, but never calls them virtues or asks whether they can sit alongside moderation. Marla only talks about moderation itself. With no position from either side, this can''t be the disagreement.'),
13	('415cc118-5333-43af-af8d-e2e49d451f1b'::uuid,'675880a0e19ba285592cd7521e26b635','This sounds close, because both speakers talk about leaving the middle course. But neither gives a frequency or says how often a person ou...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('48db2e4a-ddf3-48ca-b47e-83cf548f3ec8'::uuid,'0a0c976ee4c09b47d542216f7ac5d362','Public figures'' private correspondence and diaries are meant to be confidential when written, but they later become biographers'' principal sources. In the future, most such documents are likely to be stored as encrypted text, which can be opened by password only.','Evidence','Evidence'),
3	('b1263c88-23c0-4741-b8db-d358aca86a38'::uuid,'d0c541810b81496af94848989e808dff','On Main Conclusion, read with one expectation in mind: the conclusion usually arrives before its support. Most of the time it is the first sentence or the sentence right after a pivot like "however."
4	
5	So test the first sentence. It is a prediction: encryption "will bring the writing of biographical history virtually to an end." Nobody can observe the future, so a prediction is an opinion, and an opinion needs support. Ask: why should we believe that? The rest of the paragraph answers. Diaries and letters are the biographers'' main sources. Those documents will likely be encrypted. So historians without passwords will lose the best material. Every later sentence is a reason for the first one.
6	
7	Notice the chain has two levels. The "so" claim (historians will lose the revealing material) is itself a conclusion, supported by the encryption premise. But the argument does not stop there. Losing that material matters because it ends biographical writing. When one conclusion supports another, the one that supports nothing further is the main conclusion.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
8	('8fc0988e-af43-4bb4-9943-667e173bd4fd'::uuid,'687611a9181081b7f4e8dd4a9dc04404','Find a meaning match for the first sentence: widespread encryption will bring the writing of biographical history virtually to an end. "Virtually to an end" means almost stopping, so a matching answer can say it will nearly stop, sharply decline, or cease.
9	
10	Expect traps that restate the encryption premise or the "so" claim about historians losing access. Both sit on the path to the conclusion, but neither is where the argument ends up.','Goal','Goal'),
11	('5b615769-97c6-4cf3-890c-223b39d4fef0'::uuid,'5546e0e4718a5113c19f0ecf86f92778','Radioactive elements are unstable, so most of them decay within at most a few million years into other, nonradioactive elements. Yet there is an abundance of radioactive elements in the universe today.','Evidence','Evidence'),
12	('45dbc0d9-a286-424f-a410-af0ebba07c3e'::uuid,'f14c81b3b5a374fdad0d680b2f157c46','On Main Conclusion, expect the conclusion before its support. It is usually the first sentence or the sentence after a pivot.
13	
14	The first sentence hedges: radioactive elements "may have been created when the universe began." Here that "may" claim is a concession, not something the author sets out to prove. Then the pivot: "However, even if this occurred, these elements are clearly still being created in the universe today." "Clearly" marks the author''s own view. Check it with the support test: why should we believe new radioactive elements are being created?
15	
16	The rest of the paragraph answers that question. Most radioactive elements decay within a few million years. So if none had been made since the beginning, almost none would be left. But there are plenty left. Lined up:
17	
18	No new elements created after the beginning → almost none left today
19	Not almost none left (there is an abundance)
20	So: new elements have been created
21	
22	That is the contrapositive at work, and it lands exactly on the second sentence.
23	
24	The final sentence starts with "So," which tempts us to call it the conclusion. It is a conclusion, but an intermediate one: it is drawn from the decay premise and then used to prove the second sentence. On Main Conclusion, the last sentence is rarely the answer, so check it before you trust it.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
25	('700bf360...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

2	('5716c1d9-c32d-47a3-90b2-be88d637aae9'::uuid,'466e77c47999b5c3735e4666df44618d','This is the premise about storage: "most such documents are likely to be stored as encrypted text." Run the support test. Why does the author think biography will end? Partly because of this. A reason offered for the conclusion is evidence, not the conclusion. A also hardens "likely" into "will be."'),
3	('28dd4f0c-3a92-4e30-8dbb-d279c9f10308'::uuid,'212e13afe894a4d0f59bac205c4ec4c2','This matches the first sentence piece by piece. "widespread use of encryption software" carries over unchanged. "will bring the writing of biographical history virtually to an end" becomes "will cause the writing of biographical history to decline significantly or cease." An almost-end is a significant decline, and an actual end is ceasing, so B covers the author''s prediction without adding or dropping anything.
4	
5	The move to repeat: once you have located the conclusion, put it in plainer words and look for the answer that says the same thing. On Main Conclusion, the correct answer is usually a paraphrase of one sentence, not a new idea.'),
6	('521a059e-21a5-453f-a0c6-7dd5511d7a25'::uuid,'48febcd85701c3bc0751a8bafd1d50ec','This is close to the "so" claim: without passwords, historians lose the most revealing material. That claim is supported by the encryption premise, and it in turn supports the first sentence. Ask what it is for. Historians losing access is the reason biography will end. A claim that supports something else in the paragraph is not the main conclusion.'),
7	('e44d8779-ecc1-47c9-9c5d-c39104e57583'::uuid,'34339b91a0086bf354f53546076fe23c','This is the last-claim trap. D restates the final sentence: the most interesting, revealing material "will be unavailable to historians unless they have the necessary passwords." It feels like the conclusion because it follows "so," and it is a conclusion, but only an intermediate one.
8	
9	Run the support test in both directions. Why should we believe biography will end? Because historians will lose the best material without passwords. Why should we believe historians will lose it? Because the documents will be encrypted. The password claim sits in the middle of that chain. The first sentence is the only claim that is supported and supports nothing further, so it is the main conclusion.'),
10	('e07233f4-c780-4715-9e57-d3fe27d6d275'::uuid,'d38f323b0e21270711942411a6967edc','The stimulus calls these documents biographers'' "principal sources" and implies the most revealing material sits in them. E turns that into a ranking of all sources, which the author never states. Even as an implied idea, it would be background that explains why losing the documents matters. It supports the prediction; it is not the prediction.'),
11	('4944ceab-0422-4199-8e02-7edc0a8977b8'::uuid,'75ef81343ce2b61a035c96db6400b9ac','The argument never states this. 12	('3d908710-dcdc-42cf-939a-8e8dee7b371f'::uuid,'3e76e66bd0673a615e2cac5229a8c545','This is the second sentence with the pronoun swapped out. "these elements" means radioactive elements, and "are clearly still being created in the universe today" becomes "are being created in the universe today." Dropping "clearly" and "still" does not change the claim.
13

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('4d006c9f-2adb-4b45-ad0a-c61058ef5e47'::uuid,'e2632d05168ef65add8ae905acc978f3','Line up what the psychologists studied against what the author concludes.
3	
4	The research is about one schedule. Children in nine-month schools forget a significant amount during summer breaks. That''s it. Nothing in the evidence is about the twelve-month schedule. Yet the author claims that schedule "will insure" students do not forget during their breaks.
5	
6	Where does that guarantee come from? The author is assuming forgetting is a problem of the nine-month schedule only. But the twelve-month schedule still has breaks. Three of them, each a month long.
7	
8	Given that kids forget over a long summer, how could we argue the twelve-month plan is no better? Maybe kids forget plenty in a month, too. If a month away wipes out most of what a summer away does, the twelve-month schedule could mean three rounds of forgetting instead of one.
9	
10	So the gap is in the comparison. The evidence shows a bad result in one situation, says nothing about the other, and the author treats the other as free of it.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
11	('730ec4b7-2e8c-497f-b24e-11ad4b771c51'::uuid,'0fc0d08152cad15a92f7806b20bb18e5','Look for an answer saying the argument assumes, without evidence, that forgetting during breaks happens only under the nine-month schedule. A "fails to consider" version would also work: the argument fails to consider that students might forget during month-long breaks too. Either way, the answer should point at the missing evidence about the twelve-month schedule.','Goal','Goal'),
12	('aac5aeae-90fe-4f43-975e-7342189649ab'::uuid,'d2efdc0e0e243e46187ca775840d0b4a','Watch the word "crime" change jobs between the evidence and the conclusion.
13	
14	The evidence is about classification. Vagrancy laws turn "innocuous everyday occurrences" into crimes. Acts that were legal yesterday are illegal today.
15	
16	The conclusion is about amount. Vagrancy laws "increase crime." The columnist sets this against the laws'' purpose of reducing "criminal activity," which makes it sound like people are now doing more harmful things.
17	
18	Picture a park where twenty people nap on benches every week. A vagrancy law passes. The same twenty people nap on the same benches. On paper, the town now has twenty crimes a week that it didn''t have before. Has anyone''s behavior changed? No. The count went up because the label changed.
19	
20	So the evidence supports only "more acts are now called crimes." The conclusion needs "people are committing more crime." The columnist slides from one claim to the other as if they were the same.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
21	('e242526d-7b83-4e28-97dd-4583986280a8'::uuid,'704cb2b38b8e165d060e6de25fc7444a','Look for an answer saying the argument confuses relabeling acts as crimes with an actual increase in criminal activity. It might say the argument fails to distinguish the two, or that it uses "crime" in two different senses.','Goal','Goal'),
22	('be542e31-fdb8-4f27-9efd-47a6c021fbd1'::uuid,'a06f0987e29377977b61d5e19435ecad','The evidence is a conditional. Fresh okra is required for the best seafood gumbo:
23	
24	best gumbo → fresh okra
25	
26	Its contrapositive tells us what happens without fresh okra:
27	
28	no fresh okra → no best gumbo
29	
30	That''s what the evidence supports. Now read the conclusion closely. Without reliable fresh okra, customers could not count on good gumbo:
31	
32	no fresh okra → no good gumbo
33	
34	"Best" became "good." Those are different standards. A gumbo can be good without being the best. Without fresh okra, Chris might still serve a very good gumbo that just isn''t the best one possible.
35

input: {"project_id":"53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76","sql":"update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values\n('4d006c9f-2adb-4b45-ad0a-c61058ef5e47'::uuid,'e2632d05168ef65add8ae905acc978f3','Line up what the psychologists studied against what the author concludes.\n\nThe research is about one schedule. Children in nine-month schools forget a significant amount during summer breaks. That''s it. Nothing in the evidence is about the twelve-month schedule. Yet the author claims that schedule \"will insure\" students do not forget during their breaks.\n\nWhere does that guarantee come from? The author is assuming forgetting is a problem of the nine-month schedule only. But the twelve-month schedule still has breaks. Three of them, each a month long.\n\nGiven that kids forget over a long summer, how could we argue the twelve-month plan is no better? Maybe kids forget plenty in a month, too. If a month away wipes out most of what a summer away does, the twelve-month schedule could mean three rounds of forgetting instead of one.\n\nSo the gap is in the comparison. The evidence shows a bad result in one situation, says nothing about the other, and the author treats the other as free of it.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),\n('730ec4b7-2e8c-497f-b24e-11ad4b771c51'::uuid,'0fc0d08152cad15a92f7806b20bb18e5','Look for an answer saying the argument assumes, without evidence, that forgetting during breaks happens only under the nine-month schedule. A \"fails to consider\" version would also work: the argument fails to consider that students might forget during month-long breaks too. Either way, the answer should point at the missing evidence about the twelve-month schedule.','Goal','Goal'),\n('aac5aeae-90fe-4f43-975e-7342189649ab'::uuid,'d2efdc0e0e243e46187ca775840d0b4a','Watch the word \"crime\" change jobs between the evidence and the conclusion.\n\nThe evidence is about classification. 2	('e34d0838-18d8-4ccd-8b0d-337355c43b69'::uuid,'015f118d9aba35d7caec6c561e823e14','The argument never weighs academic against nonacademic learning. It limits itself on purpose: the twelve-month schedule is preferred "insofar as academic learning is concerned." Test the presumption: if academic and nonacademic experiences were worth exactly the same, would the argument lose anything? No. It needs no view about their comparative worth.'),
3	('3549c011-a0fb-4d8c-a7fc-5134a91b642c'::uuid,'87e03991eaade35120dff6bd3051b4ee','The two groups here, children on a nine-month schedule and children on a twelve-month schedule, differ in exactly the way the argument cares about: how the breaks are arranged. That distinction is the subject of the argument, not an arbitrary line. The problem is what the author assumes about one side, not where the line is drawn.'),
4	('abf5c035-ed10-43f4-80e2-0a928c7019d1'::uuid,'f82a6cd5a82303244c2d2290de63db38','This matches the prediction in formal words. The "two situations" are the two schedules. The "undesirable result" is forgetting schooling over a break. The evidence ties that result to the nine-month schedule, and the author takes for granted that it belongs to that schedule alone, so the month-long breaks are assumed to be safe.
5	
6	When a comparison has evidence about only one option, check whether the argument quietly assumes the other option is free of the problem. That unstated "only" is what an answer like this one names.'),
7	('5e9e56b6-b330-429a-a95c-1978c233a16e'::uuid,'653a4aadac0c1a76d1592bea47c6cf31','Questioning the sample attacks the one part of the argument that has evidence behind it. The research only needs to describe children in nine-month schools, and nothing suggests it doesn''t. Grant that the data is perfectly representative and the argument still fails, because no one has shown that month-long breaks avoid the same forgetting.'),
8	('0e57e7f7-876d-47a4-86e7-37a3d9d78a20'::uuid,'ca8bcd00c1fb88d9b67cd06bdcf2efff','The author accepts the psychologists'' finding and uses it as the main evidence. That view is never rejected later in the argument. If anything, the argument leans on the research too far, by stretching a finding about summer breaks into a promise about month-long breaks.'),
9	('32e01438-bb80-4728-81ee-0a40814e08e8'::uuid,'699b6e128479874c1493b4ce8a40b18d','The columnist calls these occurrences "innocuous," which means harmless. The whole complaint is that harmless acts get treated as crimes. So the argument presumes the opposite of this answer: it treats the occurrences as not harmful.'),
10	('0d6f50c3-bbf9-4503-879d-0310cf72cd97'::uuid,'02d66b588d488920601bb03bfa42e3e1','The columnist never says vagrancy laws produce more innocuous occurrences. The occurrences stay the same; only their legal status changes. This answer invents an increase in the occurrences and then criticizes a causal inference the argument never makes.'),
11	('9a7649fd-02d9-45ac-9956-c066c97c575e'::uuid,'e2a39a136a473d1df894c86f45d74ffd','Ask whether naming the occurrences would fix anything. Suppose the columnist listed them: napping on a bench, standing on a corner. The argument would still slide from "more acts count as crimes" to "more criminal activity." The vagueness isn''t what breaks the argument.'),
12	('83218fb4-db52-490b-8e73-95df524be292'::uuid,'c52784f6e1fe090028e9fa43f9d35164','On a "fails to consider" answer, ask whether the idea would weaken. Suppose crime would rise even without vagrancy laws. That says nothing about whether the laws themselves add to the count, which is the columnist''s claim. Other causes of crime leave the argument untouched.'),
13	('ce414672-98b3-4b32-8fdd-fb51629899a8'::uuid,'0c86ef2a3b7af05aec370eccd92d0ef2','This names the slide we predicted. "An increase in criminal activity" is what the conclusion claims. "The reclassification of certain occurrences as crimes" is all the evidence shows....
