3	('9079926a-ee5e-4a51-8546-5c6f2cb2cb00'::uuid,'95788adb889f2c37af3e6714c0eef4d1','It is a given that to be an intriguing person, one must be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others.
4	
5	Intriguing → Inspires others'' curiosity
6	
7	This explains why inspiring curiosity matters. It is not offered as support for the claim that follows.','Problem','Context'),
8	('53dc91b2-d899-497d-92c0-bbf5821c8dc3'::uuid,'0620e6fa99dbd32aab8ac497ae931c19','Constantly broadening one''s abilities and extending one''s intellectual reach [perpetually expanding one''s mind] will enable one to inspire that curiosity.
9	
10	Expands mind → Inspires others'' curiosity','Conclusion','Conclusion'),
11	('633d69ab-fbf0-4cef-acfa-3b1535d1fdcb'::uuid,'f723ce5ad9e123bd7aa8873cc5a26a0f','For such a perpetual expansion of one''s mind makes it impossible to be fully comprehended, making one a constant mystery to others.
12	
13	Expands mind → Not fully comprehended → Constant mystery','Premise','Evidence (For)'),
14	('dbdd8c0f-ffab-4046-b111-38329719d11b'::uuid,'d46231313a83670f5e5239d3d60f36e2','Many people think that if human language evolved, then something like it must be present in those species most closely related to humans, such as chimpanzees.','Opposing Evidence','Opposing Point'),
15	('60c18f69-02c7-4cfc-bd71-ecc476c46302'::uuid,'7aaa39e01db7d479d3ab9eec9f2dc820','They reason that since new traits evolve gradually, something like human language, albeit cruder, must exist in some species from which humans evolved.','Opposing Point','Opposing Evidence'),
16	('f649ca28-ceb7-4917-9196-ccc1cdf772f1'::uuid,'245b6efe6652ffc783002123f1f3a62d','This general line of argument may be reasonable, but it simply does not follow that chimpanzees have anything like human language.','Evidence','Conclusion'),
17	('742572f9-a5a5-45c3-902a-e374fcf00f4d'::uuid,'64dae0e7fc68e6adf6ed00ad6efefac2','Humans did not evolve from chimpanzees. Chimpanzees are closely related to humans only because both evolved from a common ancestor, and the evolution of human language might easily have begun after the extinction of that common ancestor.','Conclusion','Evidence'),
18	('faeec81a-8f99-4993-b190-fdb631d6d733'::uuid,'9c7a82eee8d8bb9bac6710d9e1be18d1','The reason is that reforms are generally undertaken early in a new government.
19	(*why should we believe that?*)','Intermediate Conclusion','Intermediate Conclusion'),
20	('17884611-816c-465a-97e6-8a2062b1a24f'::uuid,'129beca1aa6c5fed133bba8ce0f54e8a','If leaders don''t act quickly to solve a problem and it becomes an issue later, they must either deny there is a problem or deny that anything could have been done about it. Otherwise, they have to admit responsibility for the problem persisting. Waiting puts leaders in that bind, which is why reform tends to come early.','Evidence','Evidence'),
21	('37b696bf-f5fe-452b-80bb-a04d449faf90'::uuid,'ef9b4386c4a3146c2eaefd26a4b41ef2','Whenever we''re doing Main Conclusion, we prime our brain before reading the paragraph: "Remember, on Main Conclusion and Role, the conclusion usually appears **earlier** than its support."
22	
23	For these questions, the conclusion is usually
24	- the first sentence
25	or
26	- a rebuttal
27	
28	When we read this first sentence, we''re asking ourselves, "Is this the author''s conclusion? Does it sound like the pundit''s opinion?"
29	
30	The normative language "it is good to X" tells us that the first sentence is an opinion. If the rest of the paragraph tries to support that claim, then we''ll know that the first sentence was the main conclusion.
31	
32	The second sentence telegraphs that support is being provided by saying "the **reason** is t...

input: {"project_id":"53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76","sql":"update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values\n('96be1514-f9da-47c9-9782-e02eb3ea4590'::uuid,'bf1d923a7bd3b8f250576eb536dcfae5','Contrary to recent speculations, no hardware store will be opening in the shopping plaza.','Conclusion','Conclusion'),\n('9079926a-ee5e-4a51-8546-5c6f2cb2cb00'::uuid,'95788adb889f2c37af3e6714c0eef4d1','It is a given that to be an intriguing person, one must be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others.\n\nIntriguing → Inspires others'' curiosity\n\nThis explains why inspiring curiosity matters. It is not offered as support for the claim that follows.','Problem','Context'),\n('53dc91b2-d899-497d-92c0-bbf5821c8dc3'::uuid,'0620e6fa99dbd32aab8ac497ae931c19','Constantly broadening one''s abilities and extending one''s intellectual reach [perpetually expanding one''s mind] will enable one to inspire that curiosity.\n\nExpands mind → Inspires others'' curiosity','Conclusion','Conclusion'),\n('633d69ab-fbf0-4cef-acfa-3b1535d1fdcb'::uuid,'f723ce5ad9e123bd7aa8873cc5a26a0f','For such a perpetual expansion of one''s mind makes it impossible to be fully comprehended, making one a constant mystery to others.\n\nExpands mind → Not fully comprehended → Constant mystery','Premise','Evidence (For)'),\n('dbdd8c0f-ffab-4046-b111-38329719d11b'::uuid,'d46231313a83670f5e5239d3d60f36e2','Many people think that if human language evolved, then something like it must be present in those species most closely related to humans, such as chimpanzees.','Opposing Evidence','Opposing Point'),\n('60c18f69-02c7-4cfc-bd71-ecc476c46302'::uuid,'7aaa39e01db7d479d3ab9eec9f2dc820','They reason that since new traits evolve gradually, something like human language, albeit cruder, must exist in some species from which humans evolved.','Opposing Point','Opposing Evidence'),\n('f649ca28-ceb7-4917-9196-ccc1cdf772f1'::uuid,'245b6efe6652ffc783002123f1f3a62d...
[/external_agent_tool_call]

1	insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values
2	('5dab36d2-be42-4dac-b4c3-9a1758a48326'::uuid,2,'Evaluate','On Main Conclusion questions, listen for the conclusion early. It usually shows up in the first sentence or right after a pivot that pushes back on someone else''s view.
3	
4	Here the first sentence does both. "Contrary to recent speculations" tells us other people expect a hardware store, and the author is about to disagree. Then comes the author''s own claim: no hardware store will be opening in the plaza.
5	
6	Test it by asking, "Why should I believe that?" The next two sentences answer. Anyone opening a store there would already be publicizing it, and there has been no publicity. So nobody is opening a store there. Both sentences point back at the first one, which makes the first sentence the main conclusion and the other two its evidence.
7	
8	The speculation itself is not the author''s view. It is the position the author argues against.'),
9	('5dab36d2-be42-4dac-b4c3-9a1758a48326'::uuid,3,'Goal','Find the answer that restates the author''s claim with the opener stripped off: a hardware store will not be opening in the shopping plaza. Expect trap answers that restate the speculation, the conditional premise, or the no-publicity premise.'),
10	('c97829cb-ce32-4ed7-b348-87a1a99191f7'::uuid,3,'Evaluate','The conclusion here sits in the middle sentence, and the word "For" is what gives it away. "For" means "because": the last sentence is the reason for the sentence before it. Why should we believe that expanding your mind lets you inspire curiosity? Because it makes you impossible to fully comprehend, so you stay a mystery to others.
11	
12	So what is the first sentence doing? It is called "a given." Nothing argues for it, and it is not a reason for the middle sentence either. It explains why anyone would care about inspiring curiosity. That makes it background, not the conclusion.
13	
14	Now the hard part: the conclusion is a conditional, so direction matters. The author says expanding your mind is enough to inspire curiosity:
15	
16	Expands mind → Inspires others'' curiosity
17	
18	The author does not say it is the only way. An answer that flips this into a requirement, or that changes whose curiosity we are talking about, is a different claim.'),
19	('c97829cb-ce32-4ed7-b348-87a1a99191f7'::uuid,4,'Goal','Look for an answer that says: if you constantly broaden your abilities and extend your intellectual reach, you will be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others. Check the direction (broadening is the trigger, curiosity is the result) and check that the curiosity belongs to other people.'),
20	('737e7000-49dd-46b2-b774-be961f17f175'::uuid,2,'Evaluate','On Main Conclusion questions, check the first sentence first. It is often the claim, with the reasons following it.
21	
22	The manager opens with an opinion: there is no good reason to suppose promoting creativity is a proper goal of employee training. Ask, "Why should I believe that?" The rest of the paragraph answers. Reason one: many jobs need little or no creativity, and in those jobs creativity is more likely to disrupt than to innovate. Reason two, flagged by "Furthermore": even if creativity were in demand, there is no evidence it can be taught.
23	
24	"Furthermore" stacks a second reason on the first. Both point back at the opening sentence, so that sentence is the main conclusion.
25	
26	Notice how careful the conclusion is. The manager does not say training should never promote creativity. The claim is only that there is no good reason to think it should.'),
27	('737e7000-49dd-46b2-b774-be961f17f175'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer that matches the first sentence, including its caution: there is no good reason to suppose employee training programs should promote creativity. Expect traps that restate one of the two reasons, or that state them more strongly than the manager did.'),
28	(...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

17	('02391206-401d-4b1c-9c7b-de7462a9c9ee'::uuid,'9a28ab4c03556c4c84e820ca5ae373ed','D reverses the conclusion. The author says expanding your mind is enough to inspire curiosity. D says it is required: to inspire curiosity, one must constantly broaden.

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('49a6cf2d-3ad4-48a5-805a-d29fd8627b5f'::uuid,'1689a34952a2fd21858ef29706c6c9b0','The blank comes after "since", so it is the conclusion, and everything before it is the evidence. Let''s line up what happens to the workers.
3	
4	Mass production lowered prices by using fewer workers, many of whom needed little training. Lower prices let workers buy goods they could not afford before. That''s a gain.
5	
6	Then the pivot: "But since" jobs needing little training are more vulnerable to elimination. That''s a cost, and it lands on exactly the kind of job mass production relies on.
7	
8	So one cause, mass production, does two things to the same people. It raises what they can buy, and it makes their jobs less secure. The "But" tells us the conclusion should bring in the downside without erasing the upside.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
9	('61d0699b-ae2f-4f00-8933-8c2be2ded79f'::uuid,'8b9a0c23e6c20db17fc413cb809448b1','Predict a modest answer that ties the two together: the same thing that boosted workers'' buying power also left their jobs more exposed. Be wary of answers that compare groups the stimulus never compares, bring in new topics such as profits, or turn a vulnerability into job losses that have already happened.','Goal','Goal'),
10	('90bd8980-9a76-4618-9087-855534bd9189'::uuid,'fef9249ddaf26ac57bea76be29cbb9f4','Start with the structure. "Although" opens a concession: a single application gives short-term gains. The main point will push against that. "We should conclude that" marks the blank as the conclusion, and the sentence just before it, that long-term use gradually depresses yield, is the evidence driving it.
11	
12	Now read that evidence closely. Yields go up at first, then slide down "from this initially elevated level". It doesn''t say yields fall below where they started. It says nothing about costs, the environment, or the pests themselves. So the safe conclusion is about a payoff that shrinks over time, not one that disappears or does harm.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
13	('f498eaa0-c6c2-4cbc-a1ce-0f94331a4594'::uuid,'6cbf8f1d8eb38321d38d2096484fce99','Predict something like this: the benefit of these pesticides fades with continued use. On a completion like this, reject anything stronger than a fading benefit (a net loss, an out-of-control pest problem) and anything that brings in a topic the stimulus never raised (the environment, the instructions on the label).','Goal','Goal'),
14	('415fba4f-484e-4b5c-9ee4-d1a76847e01f'::uuid,'f30b129479cf8cadaf3adead653c531d','  - *Contrast:* mu mesons (MMs) generated by cosmic rays just outside Earth''s atmosphere travel at speeds approaching the speed of light, **whereas** MMs generated in the lab are nearly at rest.
15	  - *Comparison:* a lab MM typically decays in much less time than it takes an MM to travel from just outside the atmosphere to our detector on Earth.
16	  - *Conditional:* if fast MMs typically decay as fast as lab MMs, then we should detect only about one one-hundredth of the number **we actually do detect.**','Given','Given'),
17	('999a1eb1-7e4b-4d8e-84b2-85a83ef89eef'::uuid,'8bdcf002c72ee1e62ecde6af54a3c5d5','Let''s call mu mesons MMs. The fast ones are made just outside the atmosphere and travel to Earth at nearly the speed of light. The lab ones are nearly at rest, and they typically decay in much less time than the trip from the upper atmosphere to our detector takes.
18	
19	The fourth sentence does the real work. It''s a conditional:
20	
21	fast MMs decay as fast as lab MMs → we detect about 1/100 of the number we actually detect
22	
23	Read the right side carefully. We can''t detect one one-hundredth of the number we actually detect. We detect the number we actually detect. So the right side is false, and when the right side of a conditional is false, the left side has to be false too. That''s the contrapositive:
24	
25	NOT detect 1/100 of t...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

input: {"project_id":"53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76","sql":"update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values\n('49a6cf2d-3ad4-48a5-805a-d29fd8627b5f'::uuid,'1689a34952a2fd21858ef29706c6c9b0','The blank comes after \"since\", so it is the conclusion, and everything before it is the evidence. Let''s line up what happens to the workers.\n\nMass production lowered prices by using fewer workers, many of whom needed little training. Lower prices let workers buy goods they could not afford before. That''s a gain.\n\nThen the pivot: \"But since\" jobs needing little training are more vulnerable to elimination. That''s a cost, and it lands on exactly the kind of job mass production relies on.\n\nSo one cause, mass production, does two things to the same people. It raises what they can buy, and it makes their jobs less secure. The \"But\" tells us the conclusion should bring in the downside without erasing the upside.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),\n('61d0699b-ae2f-4f00-8933-8c2be2ded79f'::uuid,'8b9a0c23e6c20db17fc413cb809448b1','Predict a modest answer that ties the two together: the same thing that boosted workers'' buying power also left their jobs more exposed. Be wary of answers that compare groups the stimulus never compares, bring in new topics such as profits, or turn a vulnerability into job losses that have already happened.','Goal','Goal'),\n('90bd8980-9a76-4618-9087-855534bd9189'::uuid,'fef9249ddaf26ac57bea76be29cbb9f4','Start with the structure. \"Although\" opens a concession: a single application gives short-term gains. The main point will push against that. \"We should conclude that\" marks the blank as the conclusion, and the sentence just before it, that long-term use gradually depresses yield, is the evidence driving it.\n\nNow read that evidence closely. Yields go up at first, then slide down \"from this initially elevated level\". It doesn''t say yields fall below where they started. It says nothing...
[/external_agent_tool_call]

1	update lr_explanation_choice_notes t set body=v.nb from (values
2	('6ced6357-3ec5-411d-b528-71cfd29da3ae'::uuid,'872b493bb2cdd2465489030bf8e0ddd6','The stimulus never compares the purchasing power of highly trained workers with that of less-trained workers. The lower prices helped "workers" in general. Training level shows up only in the claim about job security, so A takes a variable from one premise and attaches it to a different outcome.'),
3	('9d8e7cd8-046a-44e4-9480-6bcc33cdce85'::uuid,'73ca18e2d8258d5dd5c63babd5744ae8','Two problems. The stimulus says workers gained something: they could buy goods they could not afford before, so "decreased benefits" ignores the one benefit we''re told about. And profits for owners never appear; the stimulus says owners could lower prices, not that they earned more. B needs facts the premises don''t give.'),
4	('154ab80a-e668-47f5-91bf-97f8ec1f16c7'::uuid,'2745ea0f9e2ae1866ac8585aab09ed7f','The only comparison about job security says highly trained workers are less vulnerable than workers with little training. That points toward better security for the trained, not toward "never" having security. The stimulus says nothing about "the highest paid employees" at all, and "never" is far stronger than a comparison can support.'),
5	('9f890e38-0bef-4493-8ae7-62f06ab0c2f3'::uuid,'2016ef113a267375d6bad11a588156f9','Match it to the prediction piece by piece. "a source of increased purchasing power" is mass production and the lower prices it brought. "also undermines their job security" is the pivot: those same techniques rely on workers with little training, and those jobs are more vulnerable to elimination. D keeps both halves the stimulus set up, and it stays modest. The word "undermines" only says security is weakened, which is what "more vulnerable to elimination" gives us. On a completion that follows a "But", look for the answer that holds the gain and the cost together instead of picking one side.'),
6	('2946bed1-b948-4443-811c-2afcc367992f'::uuid,'6f9f3fc5cfbf0276679c6644c82a4266','This is the tempting overreach. "more vulnerable to elimination" describes a risk, not jobs that have actually disappeared. To say the share of workers who can afford these goods is "shrinking", we''d need real job losses, and we''d need them to outweigh the lower prices. Test it: could every worker still have a job and still afford the goods? Yes, so E isn''t supported.'),
7	('7e2a9b81-ee94-424d-a5c3-f552c52c4924'::uuid,'e6d56e6c101407f319bafce27787bd87','This tempts because it also turns the yield story into money. But "uneconomic" means the pesticides cost more than they return. We only know yield slides down from an elevated level; it could stay above the no-pesticide yield forever, and we have no figures on what the pesticides cost. A shrinking benefit is still a benefit, so A goes further than the evidence.'),
8	('83a3ece8-e662-4e6d-a2f2-850fb5c50ca9'::uuid,'c98a03e153d0d9e78bd258b17ac56e0d','The stimulus is only about crop yield. Environmental damage is a new topic, and nothing in the premises tells us anything about it. Pesticides may well harm the environment in real life, but a completion has to follow from what the stimulus says, not from what we know elsewhere.'),
9	('bd6d008c-6e20-4c93-96a2-1e91ea55b8e1'::uuid,'177307430b2a654c605959ca57515106','Pests never come up; the evidence is about yield. And "unmanageable" is extreme. A gradual decline in yield from a high point fits perfectly well with pests being fully under control, so C adds both a new topic and a much stronger claim than the evidence allows.'),
10	('9ed37f3e-07e3-4bc4-8df4-06738ef239c2'::uuid,'5a0e4cdd97a85fb8a895a7af390ff7cf','The stimulus says nothing about what the manufacturers recommend, so we can''t judge whether farmers follow it. Yields could decline even when the pesticides are used exactly as directed. "probably" softens the claim, but softening an unsupported idea doesn''t make it supported.'),
11	('022f6e42-13ae-4db2-8c...
